# Explanations

Generated by `pipeline/15-explanations.mjs` from `graph/explanation-subjects.json` and the explanation files in `content/` (docs/EXPLANATIONS-PLAN.md). Every sentence quotes an allowed source; an independent AI agent checked each one and step 09 found each quote word for word. Nothing here has been reviewed by a person yet unless it says "reviewed".

Limits: Plain 2–4 sentences at Flesch–Kincaid grade 10 or below; Core 5–10 sentences. The nine Human Atlas texts keep their Plain text unchanged.

## Texts

### Arterial

#### Aorta: Plain (drafted; grade 7; `content/FMA_3734/tier1.md`)

1. The aorta is the body's largest artery, a blood vessel that carries blood away from the heart. *(defines “artery”)*
   > “The aorta is the largest artery in the body” ([20.5 Circulatory Pathways](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-5-circulatory-pathways), CC BY 4.0)
   > “artery: blood vessel that conducts blood away from the heart” ([Chapter 20 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood Vessels and Circulation)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. Its branches carry blood to almost every organ of the body.
   > “The aorta and its branches—the systemic arteries—send blood to virtually every organ of the body” ([20.5 Circulatory Pathways](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-5-circulatory-pathways), CC BY 4.0)
3. It receives oxygen-rich blood from the left ventricle, the main pumping chamber on the heart's lower left side. *(defines “left ventricle”)*
   > “the left ventricle, which in turn pumps oxygenated blood into the aorta” ([19.1 Heart Anatomy](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-1-heart-anatomy), CC BY 4.0)
   > “the left ventricle is the major pumping chamber on the lower left side of the heart” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. It rises from the heart, curves to the left, then runs down through the chest and abdomen.
   > “The aorta ascends from the left ventricle, curves posteriorly and to the left, then descends through the thorax and abdomen.” ([SEER Training Modules: Circulatory Pathways](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/blood/pathways.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))

#### Ascending aorta: Plain (drafted; grade 6.8; `content/FMA_3736/tier1.md`)

1. The ascending aorta sends blood to the heart itself through its only branches, the coronary arteries. *(defines “coronary arteries”)*
   > “The coronary arteries are the only vessels that branch from the ascending aorta.” ([SEER Training Modules: Circulatory Pathways](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/blood/pathways.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
   > “coronary arteries: branches of the ascending aorta that supply blood to the heart” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. It is the first part of the aorta, the largest artery in the body. *(defines “aorta”)*
   > “ascending aorta: initial portion of the aorta, rising from the left ventricle for a distance of approximately 5 cm” ([Chapter 20 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood Vessels and Circulation)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “aorta: largest artery in the body” ([Chapter 20 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood Vessels and Circulation)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. It rises about 5 cm from the left ventricle, the main pumping chamber on the heart's lower left side. *(defines “left ventricle”)*
   > “ascending aorta: initial portion of the aorta, rising from the left ventricle for a distance of approximately 5 cm” ([Chapter 20 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood Vessels and Circulation)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “the left ventricle is the major pumping chamber on the lower left side of the heart” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. There the aorta turns and curves to the left, forming the aortic arch.
   > “After exiting the heart, the ascending aorta moves in a superior direction for approximately 5 cm and ends at the sternal angle. Following this ascent, it reverses direction, forming a graceful arc to the left, called the aortic arch.” ([20.5 Circulatory Pathways](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-5-circulatory-pathways), CC BY 4.0)

#### Axillary artery: Plain (drafted; grade 8.9; `content/FMA_22654/tier1.md`; also Right axillary artery, Left axillary artery)

1. The axillary artery sends blood to the brachial artery, its continuation in the upper arm. *(defines “brachial artery”)*
   > “An artery that originates from the subclavian artery at the lateral margin of the first rib that supplies the brachial artery.” ([NCIT:C32169 Axillary Artery](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C32169), CC BY 4.0)
   > “the majority of the vessel continues into the upper arm, or brachium, and becomes the brachial artery” ([20.5 Circulatory Pathways](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-5-circulatory-pathways), CC BY 4.0)
2. It also feeds the area near the top of the humerus, the upper arm bone. *(defines “humerus”)*
   > “axillary artery: continuation of the subclavian artery as it penetrates the body wall and enters the axillary region; supplies blood to the region near the head of the humerus (humeral circumflex arteries)” ([Chapter 20 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood Vessels and Circulation)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “humerus: single bone of the upper arm” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. It begins where the subclavian artery, which supplies the arms and shoulders, enters the armpit. *(defines “subclavian artery”)*
   > “As the subclavian artery exits the thorax into the axillary region, it is renamed the axillary artery.” ([20.5 Circulatory Pathways](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-5-circulatory-pathways), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Each subclavian artery supplies blood to the arms, chest, shoulders, back, and central nervous system.” ([20.5 Circulatory Pathways](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-5-circulatory-pathways), CC BY 4.0)

### Cardiac

#### Heart: Core (drafted; grade 12.9; `content/FMA_7088/tier2.md`)

1. The heart is a muscular pump whose force keeps blood circulating to every tissue in the body.
   > “The heart is a muscular pump that provides the force necessary to circulate the blood to all the tissues in the body.” ([SEER Training Modules: Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
2. It sits in the mediastinum, the space between the lungs, where a tough membrane called the pericardium separates it from the other structures there. *(defines “mediastinum”)*
   > “Within the mediastinum, the heart is separated from the other mediastinal structures by a tough membrane known as the pericardium, or pericardial sac, and sits in its own space called the pericardial cavity.” ([19.1 Heart Anatomy](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-1-heart-anatomy), CC BY 4.0)
   > “The human heart is located within the thoracic cavity, medially between the lungs in the space known as the mediastinum.” ([19.1 Heart Anatomy](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-1-heart-anatomy), CC BY 4.0)
3. It is a four-chambered organ about the size and shape of a closed fist, with two-thirds of its mass to the left of the body's midline.
   > “The human heart is a four-chambered muscular organ, shaped and sized roughly like a man's closed fist with two-thirds of the mass to the left of midline.” ([SEER Training Modules: Structure of the Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/structure.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
4. Two of its chambers, the thin-walled atria, receive blood from the veins, and the other two, the thick-walled ventricles, pump blood forcefully out of the heart. *(defines “atria”)*
   > “The two atria are thin-walled chambers that receive blood from the veins. The two ventricles are thick-walled chambers that forcefully pump blood out of the heart.” ([SEER Training Modules: Structure of the Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/structure.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
5. Blood travels from the right atrium to the right ventricle, out to the lungs to pick up oxygen, back to the left atrium, and then to the left ventricle, which pumps it into the systemic circulation, the route to the rest of the body. *(defines “systemic circulation”)*
   > “Blood flows from the right atrium to the right ventricle, and then is pumped to the lungs to receive oxygen. From the lungs, the blood flows to the left atrium, then to the left ventricle. From there it is pumped to the systemic circulation.” ([SEER Training Modules: Structure of the Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/structure.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
   > “For pulmonary and systemic circulation, the heart has to pump blood to the lungs or the rest of the body, respectively.” ([40.3 Mammalian Heart and Blood Vessels](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-3-mammalian-heart-and-blood-vessels), CC BY 4.0)
6. Two kinds of valves keep blood flowing in the right direction: atrioventricular valves between the atria and ventricles, and semilunar valves at the bases of the large vessels that leave the ventricles. *(defines “atrioventricular valves”)*
   > “The heart has two types of valves that keep the blood flowing in the correct direction. The valves between the atria and ventricles are called atrioventricular valves (also called cuspid valves), while those at the bases of the large vessels leaving the ventricles are called semilunar valves.” ([SEER Training Modules: Structure of the Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/structure.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
7. In each heartbeat, the myocardium, the thick layer of cardiac muscle cells in the heart wall, contracts in a phase called systole and then relaxes in a phase called diastole. *(defines “myocardium”)*
   > “The cardiac cycle refers to the alternating contraction and relaxation of the myocardium in the walls of the heart chambers, coordinated by the conduction system, during one heartbeat. Systole is the contraction phase of the cardiac cycle, and diastole is the relaxation phase.” ([SEER Training Modules: Physiology of the Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/physiology.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
   > “myocardium: thickest layer of the heart composed of cardiac muscle cells” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
8. The sinoatrial (SA) node, near the wall of the right atrium, is the heart's built-in pacemaker and sets the basic rhythm of the heartbeat; electrical pulses arise there on their own and make both atria contract together. *(defines “sinoatrial (SA) node”)*
   > “The internal pacemaker starts at the sinoatrial (SA) node, which is located near the wall of the right atrium. Electrical charges spontaneously pulse from the SA node causing the two atria to contract in unison.” ([40.3 Mammalian Heart and Blood Vessels](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-3-mammalian-heart-and-blood-vessels), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Because it establishes the basic rhythm of the heartbeat, it is called the pacemaker of the heart.” ([SEER Training Modules: Physiology of the Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/physiology.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
9. Each pulse then reaches the atrioventricular (AV) node, between the right atrium and right ventricle, where it pauses so the atria can empty completely into the ventricles before the ventricles pump the blood out. *(defines “atrioventricular (AV) node”)*
   > “then the pulse reaches the atrioventricular node between the right atrium and right ventricle. A pause in the electric signal allows the atria to empty completely into the ventricles before the ventricles pump out the blood.” ([40.3 Mammalian Heart and Blood Vessels](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-3-mammalian-heart-and-blood-vessels), CC BY 4.0)
   > “atrioventricular (AV) node: clump of myocardial cells located in the inferior portion of the right atrium within the atrioventricular septum; receives the impulse from the SA node, pauses, and then transmits it into specialized conducting cells within the interventricular septum” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
10. The right and left coronary arteries supply the myocardium itself with blood. *(defines “coronary arteries”)*
   > “The right and left coronary arteries, branches of the ascending aorta, supply blood to the walls of the myocardium.” ([SEER Training Modules: Structure of the Heart](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/cardiovascular/heart/structure.html), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))

### Cell types

#### Basophil: Core (drafted; grade 10.9; `content/CL_0000767/tier2.md`)

1. A basophil is a white blood cell that releases chemicals to stimulate inflammation, a basic immune response. *(defines “inflammation”)*
   > “A basophil is a leukocyte that, like a neutrophil, releases chemicals to stimulate the inflammatory response” ([42.1 Innate Immune Response](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/42-1-innate-immune-response), CC BY 4.0)
   > “inflammation: basic innate immune response characterized by heat, redness, pain, and swelling” ([Chapter 21 Key Terms (The Lymphatic and Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. It is a granular leukocyte, a white blood cell with abundant granules inside it. *(defines “granular leukocyte”)*
   > “Granular leukocytes contain abundant granules within the cytoplasm. They include neutrophils, eosinophils, and basophils” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
3. Its granules release histamine, which contributes to inflammation, and heparin, which opposes blood clotting.
   > “The granules of basophils release histamines, which contribute to inflammation, and heparin, which opposes blood clotting.” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
4. Basophils hold serotonin as well as histamine, and they release these chemicals when properly stimulated.
   > “Basophils contain vasoactive amines such as histamine and serotonin, which are released on appropriate stimulation.” ([CL:0000767 basophil](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/CL_0000767), CC BY 4.0)
5. They also produce other inflammatory chemicals that recruit more white blood cells.
   > “Eosinophils and basophils produce additional inflammatory mediators to recruit more leukocytes.” ([42.1 Innate Immune Response](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/42-1-innate-immune-response), CC BY 4.0)
6. Mast cells, immune cells that produce histamine, share the basophil's role of intensifying the inflammatory response, but the two cell types develop from different cell lines. *(defines “mast cells”)*
   > “In general, basophils intensify the inflammatory response. They share this trait with mast cells. In the past, mast cells were considered to be basophils that left the circulation. However, this appears not to be the case, as the two cell types develop from different lineages.” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
   > “mast cell: leukocyte that produces inflammatory molecules, such as histamine, in response to large pathogens and allergens” ([Chapter 42 Key Terms (The Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/42-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
7. Basophils are the least common white blood cells, typically less than one percent of the total, and measure 8–10 µm in diameter.
   > “Basophils are the least common leukocytes, typically comprising less than one percent of the total leukocyte count. They are slightly smaller than neutrophils and eosinophils at 8–10 µm in diameter.” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
8. Their large granules stain best with basic, or alkaline, stains and turn dark blue; they are so common that they can make the two-lobed nucleus hard to see.
   > “The granules of basophils stain best with basic (alkaline) stains. Basophils contain large granules that pick up a dark blue stain and are so common they may make it difficult to see the two-lobed nucleus.” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
9. Basophils arise from myeloid stem cells, which also give rise to red blood cells and some other white blood cells. *(defines “myeloid stem cells”)*
   > “Myeloid stem cells give rise to all the other formed elements, including the erythrocytes; megakaryocytes that produce platelets; and a myeloblast lineage that gives rise to monocytes and three forms of granular leukocytes: neutrophils, eosinophils, and basophils.” ([18.2 Production of the Formed Elements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-2-production-of-the-formed-elements), CC BY 4.0)

#### Cardiac muscle cell: Plain (drafted; grade 8.9; `content/CL_0000746/tier1.md`)

1. Cardiac muscle cells, also called cardiomyocytes, do the heart's pumping and make up the heart muscle.
   > “The pumping of the heart is a function of the cardiac muscle cells, or cardiomyocytes, that make up the heart muscle.” ([40.3 Mammalian Heart and Blood Vessels](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-3-mammalian-heart-and-blood-vessels), CC BY 4.0)
2. They can contract in their own built-in rhythm, with no outside signal.
   > “A principal characteristic of cardiomyocytes is that they contract on their own intrinsic rhythms without any external stimulation.” ([4.4 Muscle Tissue and Motion](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/4-4-muscle-tissue-and-motion), CC BY 4.0)
3. Neighboring cells are joined by intercalated discs, junctions that help pass electrical signals from one cell to the next. *(defines “intercalated disc”)*
   > “This line is called an intercalated disc: it assists in passing electrical impulse efficiently from one cell to the next and maintains the strong connection between neighboring cardiac cells.” ([33.2 Animal Primary Tissues](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/33-2-animal-primary-tissues), CC BY 4.0)
   > “intercalated disc: physical junction between adjacent cardiac muscle cells” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. Because the cells are linked physically and electrically, the whole heart contracts as one unit.
   > “Cardiac muscle fibers each have one to two nuclei and are physically and electrically connected to each other so that the entire heart contracts as one unit (called a syncytium).” ([10.1 Overview of Muscle Tissues](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/10-1-overview-of-muscle-tissues), CC BY 4.0)

#### Cardiac muscle cell: Core (drafted; grade 10.3; `content/CL_0000746/tier2.md`)

1. Cardiac muscle cells, or cardiomyocytes, make up the heart muscle and carry out the heart's pumping.
   > “The pumping of the heart is a function of the cardiac muscle cells, or cardiomyocytes, that make up the heart muscle.” ([40.3 Mammalian Heart and Blood Vessels](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-3-mammalian-heart-and-blood-vessels), CC BY 4.0)
2. Together they form the myocardium, the middle layer and the bulk of the heart wall. *(defines “myocardium”)*
   > “myocardium: heart muscle cells that make up the middle layer and the bulk of the heart wall” ([Chapter 40 Key Terms (The Circulatory System)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. Under a microscope they look striated, or striped, and each typically has a single nucleus in its center. *(defines “striated”)*
   > “The cells of cardiac muscle, known as cardiomyocytes, also appear striated under the microscope. Unlike skeletal muscle fibers, cardiomyocytes are single cells typically with a single centrally located nucleus.” ([4.4 Muscle Tissue and Motion](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/4-4-muscle-tissue-and-motion), CC BY 4.0)
   > “The actin and myosin proteins are arranged very regularly in the cytoplasm of individual muscle cells (referred to as fibers) in both skeletal muscle and cardiac muscle, which creates a pattern, or stripes, called striations.” ([10.1 Overview of Muscle Tissues](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/10-1-overview-of-muscle-tissues), CC BY 4.0)
4. Their plentiful mitochondria, the cell parts that produce energy, power the heart's contractions. *(defines “mitochondria”)*
   > “Mitochondria are plentiful, providing energy for the contractions of the heart.” ([19.2 Cardiac Muscle and Electrical Activity](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-2-cardiac-muscle-and-electrical-activity), CC BY 4.0)
   > “mitochondrion: one of the cellular organelles bound by a double lipid bilayer that function primarily in the production of cellular energy (ATP)” ([Chapter 3 Key Terms (The Cellular Level of Organization)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/3-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
5. The cells branch and join end to end at intercalated discs, the junctions between neighboring cardiac muscle cells. *(defines “intercalated disc”)*
   > “Cardiac muscle fibers cells also are extensively branched and are connected to one another at their ends by intercalated discs.” ([10.7 Cardiac Muscle Tissue](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/10-7-cardiac-muscle-tissue), CC BY 4.0)
   > “intercalated disc: physical junction between adjacent cardiac muscle cells” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
6. Gap junctions in the discs form channels between neighboring cells, so electrical impulses spread from cell to cell and the cells contract in a coordinated way. *(defines “gap junction”)*
   > “Gap junctions within the intercalated disks allow impulses to spread from one cardiac muscle cell to another, allowing sodium, potassium, and calcium ions to flow between adjacent cells, propagating the action potential, and ensuring coordinated contractions.” ([19.2 Cardiac Muscle and Electrical Activity](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-2-cardiac-muscle-and-electrical-activity), CC BY 4.0)
   > “A gap junction forms channels between adjacent cardiac muscle fibers” ([10.7 Cardiac Muscle Tissue](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/10-7-cardiac-muscle-tissue), CC BY 4.0)
7. The rest of each disc is made of desmosomes, structures that anchor the ends of the cells together so they do not pull apart as they contract. *(defines “desmosome”)*
   > “The remainder of the intercalated disc is composed of desmosomes. A desmosome is a cell structure that anchors the ends of cardiac muscle fibers together so the cells do not pull apart during the stress of individual fibers contracting” ([10.7 Cardiac Muscle Tissue](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/10-7-cardiac-muscle-tissue), CC BY 4.0)
8. Cardiomyocytes can also contract in their own rhythm without outside stimulation, an ability called autorhythmicity. *(defines “autorhythmicity”)*
   > “A principal characteristic of cardiomyocytes is that they contract on their own intrinsic rhythms without any external stimulation.” ([4.4 Muscle Tissue and Motion](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/4-4-muscle-tissue-and-motion), CC BY 4.0)
   > “autorhythmicity: ability of cardiac muscle to initiate its own electrical impulse that triggers the mechanical contraction that pumps blood at a fixed pace without nervous or endocrine control” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
9. Most cardiac muscle cells are myocardial contractile cells, which contract to propel blood. *(defines “myocardial contractile cells”)*
   > “myocardial contractile cells: bulk of the cardiac muscle cells in the atria and ventricles that conduct impulses and contract to propel blood” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
10. Myocardial conducting cells, the other major type, carry electrical impulses through the heart and trigger those contractions. *(defines “myocardial conducting cells”)*
   > “There are two major types of cardiac muscle cells: myocardial contractile cells and myocardial conducting cells.” ([19.2 Cardiac Muscle and Electrical Activity](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-2-cardiac-muscle-and-electrical-activity), CC BY 4.0)
   > “myocardial conducting cells: specialized cells that transmit electrical impulses throughout the heart and trigger contraction by the myocardial contractile cells” ([Chapter 19 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: The Heart)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/19-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Erythrocyte: Plain (drafted; grade 6.9; `content/CL_0000232/tier1.md`)

1. An erythrocyte, or red blood cell, carries oxygen from the lungs to the body's tissues. *(defines “erythrocyte”)*
   > “The primary functions of erythrocytes are to pick up inhaled oxygen from the lungs and transport it to the body’s tissues, and to pick up some (about 24 percent) carbon dioxide waste at the tissues and transport it to the lungs for exhalation.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “erythrocyte: (also, red blood cell) mature myeloid blood cell that is composed mostly of hemoglobin and functions primarily in the transportation of oxygen and carbon dioxide” ([Chapter 18 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. It also takes some carbon dioxide waste from the tissues back to the lungs.
   > “The primary functions of erythrocytes are to pick up inhaled oxygen from the lungs and transport it to the body’s tissues, and to pick up some (about 24 percent) carbon dioxide waste at the tissues and transport it to the lungs for exhalation.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
3. It is made in the bone marrow, the tissue inside bones, and circulates through the body. *(defines “bone marrow”)*
   > “Red blood cells, or erythrocytes (erythro- = “red”; -cyte = “cell”), are specialized cells that circulate through the body delivering oxygen to cells; they are formed from stem cells in the bone marrow.” ([40.2 Components of the Blood](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-2-components-of-the-blood), CC BY 4.0)
   > “bone marrow: tissue found inside bones; the site of all blood cell differentiation and maturation of B lymphocytes” ([Chapter 21 Key Terms (The Lymphatic and Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. It is packed with hemoglobin, a compound that carries oxygen. *(defines “hemoglobin”)*
   > “The most abundant formed elements in blood, erythrocytes are red, biconcave disks packed with an oxygen-carrying compound called hemoglobin.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “hemoglobin: oxygen-carrying compound in erythrocytes” ([Chapter 18 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Erythrocyte: Core (drafted; grade 10.7; `content/CL_0000232/tier2.md`)

1. An erythrocyte, or red blood cell, carries oxygen from the lungs to the body's tissues and brings some carbon dioxide waste back to the lungs. *(defines “erythrocyte”)*
   > “The primary functions of erythrocytes are to pick up inhaled oxygen from the lungs and transport it to the body’s tissues, and to pick up some (about 24 percent) carbon dioxide waste at the tissues and transport it to the lungs for exhalation.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “erythrocyte: (also, red blood cell) mature myeloid blood cell that is composed mostly of hemoglobin and functions primarily in the transportation of oxygen and carbon dioxide” ([Chapter 18 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. It forms in the bone marrow, the tissue inside bones, and then circulates through the body. *(defines “bone marrow”)*
   > “Red blood cells, or erythrocytes (erythro- = “red”; -cyte = “cell”), are specialized cells that circulate through the body delivering oxygen to cells; they are formed from stem cells in the bone marrow.” ([40.2 Components of the Blood](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/40-2-components-of-the-blood), CC BY 4.0)
   > “bone marrow: tissue found inside bones; the site of all blood cell differentiation and maturation of B lymphocytes” ([Chapter 21 Key Terms (The Lymphatic and Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. As it matures in the red bone marrow, it pushes out its nucleus and most other organelles, the small working parts inside a cell. *(defines “organelles”)*
   > “As an erythrocyte matures in the red bone marrow, it extrudes its nucleus and most of its other organelles.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “organelle: small structures that exist within cells and carry out cellular functions” ([Chapter 1 Key Terms (The Study of Life)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/1-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. Lacking most organelles leaves it more room inside for hemoglobin, an oxygen-carrying compound. *(defines “hemoglobin”)*
   > “Since they lack most organelles, there is more interior space for the presence of the hemoglobin molecules that, as you will see shortly, transport gases.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “hemoglobin: oxygen-carrying compound in erythrocytes” ([Chapter 18 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
5. Each hemoglobin molecule holds four heme groups, iron-containing pigments that bind oxygen, so it can carry up to four oxygen molecules. *(defines “heme”)*
   > “Each hemoglobin molecule contains four iron-containing heme molecules, and because of this, one hemoglobin molecule is capable of carrying up to four molecules of oxygen.” ([22.5 Transport of Gases](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/22-5-transport-of-gases), CC BY 4.0)
   > “heme: red, iron-containing pigment to which oxygen binds in hemoglobin” ([Chapter 18 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
6. Because it has no mitochondria, the organelles whose main job is making cellular energy, it uses none of the oxygen it carries and can deliver it all to the tissues. *(defines “mitochondria”)*
   > “Lacking mitochondria, for example, they rely on anaerobic respiration. This means that they do not utilize any of the oxygen they are transporting, so they can deliver it all to the tissues.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “mitochondrion: one of the cellular organelles bound by a double lipid bilayer that function primarily in the production of cellular energy (ATP)” ([Chapter 3 Key Terms (The Cellular Level of Organization)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/3-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
7. Its shape is a biconcave disk, which means it is plump around the edge and very thin in the center. *(defines “biconcave”)*
   > “Erythrocytes are biconcave disks; that is, they are plump at their periphery and very thin in the center” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
8. This shape gives it more surface area for exchanging gases, relative to its volume, than a sphere of similar width would have.
   > “The biconcave shape also provides a greater surface area across which gas exchange can occur, relative to its volume; a sphere of a similar diameter would have a lower surface area-to-volume ratio.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
9. Structural proteins such as spectrin let it change shape to squeeze through capillaries, the smallest blood vessels. *(defines “capillaries”)*
   > “Erythrocytes do, however, contain some structural proteins that help the blood cells maintain their unique structure and enable them to change their shape to squeeze through capillaries. This includes the protein spectrin, a cytoskeletal protein element.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “capillary: smallest of blood vessels where physical exchange occurs between the blood and tissue cells surrounded by interstitial fluid” ([Chapter 20 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood Vessels and Circulation)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/20-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
10. After up to 120 days in the blood, worn-out erythrocytes are removed by macrophages, large engulfing cells found mainly in the bone marrow, liver and spleen. *(defines “macrophages”)*
   > “Erythrocytes live up to 120 days in the circulation, after which the worn-out cells are removed by a type of myeloid phagocytic cell called a macrophage, located primarily within the bone marrow, liver, and spleen.” ([18.3 Erythrocytes](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-3-erythrocytes), CC BY 4.0)
   > “macrophage: large phagocytic cell that engulfs foreign particles and pathogens” ([Chapter 42 Key Terms (The Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/42-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Macrophage: Plain (drafted; grade 6.8; `content/CL_0000235/tier1.md`)

1. A macrophage is a large cell that engulfs foreign particles and pathogens, agents that can cause disease. *(defines “pathogens”)*
   > “macrophage: large phagocytic cell that engulfs foreign particles and pathogens” ([Chapter 42 Key Terms (The Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/42-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “pathogen: agent with the ability to cause disease” ([Chapter 21 Key Terms (Viruses)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/21-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. It also cleans up dead and damaged cells in the body.
   > “Monocytes give rise to phagocytic macrophages that clean up dead and damaged cells in the body, whether they are foreign or from the host animal.” ([33.2 Animal Primary Tissues](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/33-2-animal-primary-tissues), CC BY 4.0)
3. Macrophages live in many tissues of the body; some roam freely, while others stay fixed in place.
   > “Macrophages exist in many tissues of the body, either freely roaming through connective tissues or fixed to reticular fibers within specific tissues such as lymph nodes.” ([21.2 Barrier Defenses and the Innate Immune Response](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-2-barrier-defenses-and-the-innate-immune-response), CC BY 4.0)
4. They are monocytes, a type of white blood cell, that have left the bloodstream. *(defines “monocytes”)*
   > “Macrophages are monocytes that have left the circulation and phagocytize debris, foreign pathogens, worn-out erythrocytes, and many other dead, worn out, or damaged cells.” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
   > “monocyte: type of white blood cell that circulates in the blood and lymph and differentiates into macrophages after it moves into infected tissue” ([Chapter 42 Key Terms (The Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/42-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Macrophage: Core (drafted; grade 12; `content/CL_0000235/tier2.md`)

1. A macrophage engulfs pathogens, which are agents that can cause disease, as well as debris, worn-out red blood cells and other dead or damaged cells. *(defines “pathogens”)*
   > “Macrophages are monocytes that have left the circulation and phagocytize debris, foreign pathogens, worn-out erythrocytes, and many other dead, worn out, or damaged cells.” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
   > “pathogen: agent with the ability to cause disease” ([Chapter 21 Key Terms (Viruses)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/21-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. Macrophages are monocytes, a type of white blood cell, that have left the bloodstream. *(defines “monocytes”)*
   > “Macrophages are monocytes that have left the circulation and phagocytize debris, foreign pathogens, worn-out erythrocytes, and many other dead, worn out, or damaged cells.” ([18.4 Leukocytes and Platelets](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-4-leukocytes-and-platelets), CC BY 4.0)
   > “monocyte: type of white blood cell that circulates in the blood and lymph and differentiates into macrophages after it moves into infected tissue” ([Chapter 42 Key Terms (The Immune System)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/42-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. They live in many tissues of the body; some roam freely, while others stay fixed in place.
   > “Macrophages exist in many tissues of the body, either freely roaming through connective tissues or fixed to reticular fibers within specific tissues such as lymph nodes.” ([21.2 Barrier Defenses and the Innate Immune Response](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-2-barrier-defenses-and-the-innate-immune-response), CC BY 4.0)
4. Their names differ by tissue: Kupffer cells in the liver, alveolar macrophages in the lungs, and histiocytes in connective tissue, the tissue that holds the body's organs in place. *(defines “connective tissue”)*
   > “They are called different names, depending on the tissue: Kupffer cells in the liver, histiocytes in connective tissue, and alveolar macrophages in the lungs.” ([21.2 Barrier Defenses and the Innate Immune Response](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-2-barrier-defenses-and-the-innate-immune-response), CC BY 4.0)
   > “connective tissue: type of tissue that serves to hold in place, connect, and integrate the body’s organs and systems” ([Chapter 4 Key Terms (The Tissue Level of Organization)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/4-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
5. A macrophage has an irregular shape and moves through tissues using pseudopodia, extensions of the cell. *(defines “pseudopodia”)*
   > “A macrophage is an irregularly shaped phagocyte that is amoeboid in nature and is the most versatile of the phagocytes in the body. Macrophages move through tissues and squeeze through capillary walls using pseudopodia.” ([21.2 Barrier Defenses and the Innate Immune Response](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-2-barrier-defenses-and-the-innate-immune-response), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Still others form cytoplasmic extensions called pseudopodia anywhere on the cell, anchor the pseudopodia to a substrate, and pull themselves forward.” ([23.2 Characteristics of Protists](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/23-2-characteristics-of-protists), CC BY 4.0)
6. To engulf a pathogen, a macrophage uses phagocytosis: a section of its plasma membrane, the boundary between the cell and its surroundings, folds in around the pathogen. *(defines “plasma membrane”)*
   > “In a process that scientists call phagocytosis or endocytosis, a section of the macrophage's plasma membrane invaginates (folds in) and engulfs a pathogen.” ([4.4 The Endomembrane System and Proteins](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-4-the-endomembrane-system-and-proteins), CC BY 4.0)
   > “plasma membrane: phospholipid bilayer with embedded (integral) or attached (peripheral) proteins, and separates the cell's internal content from its surrounding environment” ([Chapter 4 Key Terms (Cell Structure)](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
7. The folded-in section pinches off with the pathogen inside and fuses with a lysosome, a part of the cell that contains digestive enzymes; these enzymes then destroy the pathogen. *(defines “lysosome”)*
   > “The invaginated section, with the pathogen inside, then pinches itself off from the plasma membrane and becomes a vesicle. The vesicle fuses with a lysosome. The lysosome’s hydrolytic enzymes then destroy the pathogen” ([4.4 The Endomembrane System and Proteins](https://openstax.org/books/biology-2e/pages/4-4-the-endomembrane-system-and-proteins), CC BY 4.0)
   > “lysosome: membrane-bound cellular organelle originating from the Golgi apparatus and containing digestive enzymes” ([Chapter 3 Key Terms (The Cellular Level of Organization)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/3-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
8. When stimulated, macrophages release cytokines, small proteins that act as chemical messengers and recruit other immune cells to infected sites. *(defines “cytokines”)*
   > “When stimulated, macrophages release cytokines, small proteins that act as chemical messengers. Cytokines recruit other cells of the immune system to infected sites and stimulate their activities.” ([4.3 Connective Tissue Supports and Protects](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/4-3-connective-tissue-supports-and-protects), CC BY 4.0)
9. They also stimulate T cells, white blood cells that physically attack foreign or diseased cells, to release cytokines that strengthen phagocytosis. *(defines “T cells”)*
   > “Macrophages stimulate T cells to release cytokines that enhance phagocytosis.” ([21.3 The Adaptive Immune Response: T lymphocytes and Their Functional Types](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/21-3-the-adaptive-immune-response-t-lymphocytes-and-their-functional-types), CC BY 4.0)
   > “T lymphocytes: (also, T cells) lymphocytes that provide cellular-level immunity by physically attacking foreign or diseased cells” ([Chapter 18 Key Terms (The Cardiovascular System: Blood)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/18-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

### Muscular

#### Abductor pollicis brevis: Plain (drafted; grade 7.4; `content/FMA_37373/tier1.md`; also Right abductor pollicis brevis, Left abductor pollicis brevis)

1. Each abductor pollicis brevis moves the thumb away from the body.
   > “Thenar muscles | Moves thumb away from body | Thumb | Abduction | Abductor pollicis brevis” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
2. This movement, called abduction, swings the thumb forward to point straight out from the palm. *(defines “abduction”)*
   > “abductor pollicis brevis: muscle that abducts the thumb” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “For the thumb, abduction is the anterior movement that brings the thumb to a 90° perpendicular position, pointing straight out from the palm.” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
3. This muscle belongs to the thenar muscles, which form the rounded bulge at the base of the thumb. *(defines “thenar muscles”)*
   > “The thenar muscles include the abductor pollicis brevis, opponens pollicis, flexor pollicis brevis, and the adductor pollicis. These muscles form the thenar eminence, the rounded contour of the base of the thumb, and all act on the thumb.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
4. Like the other thenar muscles, the abductor pollicis brevis starts and ends within the hand.
   > “They both originate and insert within the hand. The thenar muscles, which are located on the lateral part of the palm, are the abductor pollicis brevis, opponens pollicis, flexor pollicis brevis, and adductor pollicis.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)

#### Abductor pollicis longus: Plain (drafted; grade 5.8; `content/FMA_38515/tier1.md`; also Right abductor pollicis longus, Left abductor pollicis longus)

1. Each abductor pollicis longus inserts into the first metacarpal, which means the muscle moves that bone. *(defines “insert”)*
   > “abductor pollicis longus: muscle that inserts into the first metacarpal” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “insertion: end of a skeletal muscle that is attached to the structure (usually a bone) that is moved when the muscle contracts” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. The first metacarpal is the palm bone on the thumb side of the hand. *(defines “metacarpal”)*
   > “metacarpal bone: one of the five long bones that form the palm of the hand; numbered 1–5, starting on the lateral (thumb) side of the hand” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. This muscle lies deep in the back of the forearm.
   > “deep posterior compartment of the forearm: (deep posterior extensor compartment of the forearm) the abductor pollicis longus, extensor pollicis brevis, extensor pollicis longus, extensor indicis, and their associated blood vessels and nerves” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. The muscle starts on the radius and ulna, the bones of the forearm. *(defines “radius and ulna”)*
   > “The muscles of the deep posterior compartment of the forearm (deep posterior extensor compartment of the forearm) originate on the radius and ulna. These include the abductor pollicis longus, extensor pollicis brevis, extensor pollicis longus, and extensor indicis” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “forearm: region of the upper limb located between the elbow and wrist joints; contains the radius and ulna bones” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Adductor brevis: Plain (drafted; grade 8.6; `content/FMA_22442/tier1.md`; also Right adductor brevis, Left adductor brevis)

1. The adductor brevis adducts the thigh, pulling the thigh toward the body's midline. *(defines “adduct”)*
   > “adductor brevis: muscle that adducts and medially rotates the thigh” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “adduction: movement in the coronal plane that moves a limb medially toward or across the midline of the body” ([Chapter 9 Key Terms (Joints)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. The adductor brevis can also rotate the thigh inward or outward, depending on the foot's position.
   > “The adductor longus, adductor brevis, and adductor magnus can both medially and laterally rotate the thigh depending on the placement of the foot.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
3. The adductor brevis lies on the inner thigh, along with other adductor muscles.
   > “On the medial part of the thigh, the adductor longus, adductor brevis, and adductor magnus adduct the thigh and medially rotate it.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
4. In the name adductor brevis, brevis means “short.” *(defines “brevis”)*
   > “brevis: short” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Adductor longus: Plain (drafted; grade 6.9; `content/FMA_22441/tier1.md`; also Right adductor longus, Left adductor longus)

1. The adductor longus adducts the thigh, pulling the thigh toward the midline of the body. *(defines “adduct”)*
   > “adductor longus: muscle that adducts, medially rotates, and flexes the thigh” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “adduction: movement in the coronal plane that moves a limb medially toward or across the midline of the body” ([Chapter 9 Key Terms (Joints)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle also flexes the thigh, bringing the thigh forward and upward at the hip. *(defines “flex”)*
   > “The adductor longus flexes the thigh, whereas the adductor magnus extends it.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “In the lower limb, bringing the thigh forward and upward is flexion at the hip joint” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
3. In addition, the adductor longus rotates the thigh inward.
   > “adductor longus: muscle that adducts, medially rotates, and flexes the thigh” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. This muscle lies on the medial side of the thigh, the side toward the body's middle. *(defines “medial”)*
   > “On the medial part of the thigh, the adductor longus, adductor brevis, and adductor magnus adduct the thigh and medially rotate it.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “medial: describes the middle or direction toward the middle of the body” ([Chapter 1 Key Terms (An Introduction to the Human Body)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/1-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Adductor magnus: Plain (drafted; grade 6.8; `content/FMA_22443/tier1.md`; also Right adductor magnus, Left adductor magnus)

1. The adductor magnus adducts the thigh, pulling the thigh toward the body's midline. *(defines “adduct”)*
   > “On the medial part of the thigh, the adductor longus, adductor brevis, and adductor magnus adduct the thigh and medially rotate it.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “adduction: movement in the coronal plane that moves a limb medially toward or across the midline of the body” ([Chapter 9 Key Terms (Joints)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. The front part of this muscle also flexes the thigh, bringing the thigh forward and upward. *(defines “flex”)*
   > “adductor magnus: muscle with an anterior fascicle that adducts, medially rotates and flexes the thigh, and a posterior fascicle that assists in thigh extension” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “In the lower limb, bringing the thigh forward and upward is flexion at the hip joint” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
3. But the back part of the adductor magnus helps extend the thigh, moving the thigh backward. *(defines “extend”)*
   > “adductor magnus: muscle with an anterior fascicle that adducts, medially rotates and flexes the thigh, and a posterior fascicle that assists in thigh extension” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “In the lower limb, bringing the thigh forward and upward is flexion at the hip joint, while any posterior-going motion of the thigh is extension.” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
4. This muscle lies on the medial side of the thigh, the side toward the body's middle. *(defines “medial”)*
   > “On the medial part of the thigh, the adductor longus, adductor brevis, and adductor magnus adduct the thigh and medially rotate it.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “medial: describes the middle or direction toward the middle of the body” ([Chapter 1 Key Terms (An Introduction to the Human Body)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/1-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Anconeus: Plain (drafted; grade 6.7; `content/FMA_37704/tier1.md`; also Right anconeus, Left anconeus)

1. Each anconeus extends the forearm, which straightens the elbow. *(defines “extend”)*
   > “The forearm, made of the radius and ulna bones, has four main types of action at the hinge of the elbow joint: flexion, extension, pronation, and supination. The forearm flexors include the biceps brachii, brachialis, and brachioradialis. The extensors are the triceps brachii and anconeus.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “extension: movement in the sagittal plane that increases the angle of a joint (straightens the joint)” ([Chapter 9 Key Terms (Joints)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. The anconeus shares this job with the triceps brachii, a muscle at the back of the upper arm. *(defines “triceps brachii”)*
   > “The forearm flexors include the biceps brachii, brachialis, and brachioradialis. The extensors are the triceps brachii and anconeus.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Triceps brachii: in the posterior compartment of the arm” ([11.1 Interactions of Skeletal Muscles, Their Fascicle Arrangement, and Their Lever Systems](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-1-interactions-of-skeletal-muscles-their-fascicle-arrangement-and-their-lever-systems), CC BY 4.0)
3. The anconeus is a small muscle at the back of the elbow, toward the outer side.
   > “anconeus: small muscle on the lateral posterior elbow that extends the forearm” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Brachialis: Plain (drafted; grade 6.7; `content/FMA_37667/tier1.md`; also Right brachialis, Left brachialis)

1. Each brachialis adds power when you flex the forearm, or bend the elbow. *(defines “flex”)*
   > “Deep to the biceps brachii, the brachialis provides additional power in flexing the forearm.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “During forearm flexion—bending the elbow—the brachioradialis assists the brachialis.” ([11.1 Interactions of Skeletal Muscles, Their Fascicle Arrangement, and Their Lever Systems](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-1-interactions-of-skeletal-muscles-their-fascicle-arrangement-and-their-lever-systems), CC BY 4.0)
2. The brachialis lies beneath the biceps brachii, a muscle at the front of the upper arm. *(defines “biceps brachii”)*
   > “brachialis: muscle deep to the biceps brachii that provides power in flexing the forearm.” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Biceps brachii: in the anterior compartment of the arm” ([11.1 Interactions of Skeletal Muscles, Their Fascicle Arrangement, and Their Lever Systems](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-1-interactions-of-skeletal-muscles-their-fascicle-arrangement-and-their-lever-systems), CC BY 4.0)
3. The brachialis starts on the front of the humerus, the upper arm bone. *(defines “humerus”)*
   > “A muscle that originates from the lower two-thirds of the anterior surface of the humerus that flexes the elbow.” ([NCIT:C53149 Brachialis](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C53149), CC BY 4.0)
   > “humerus: single bone of the upper arm” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. When you lift a cup, the brachialis is a synergist, or helper, to the biceps brachii. *(defines “synergist”)*
   > “To lift a cup, a muscle called the biceps brachii is actually the prime mover; however, because it can be assisted by the brachialis, the brachialis is called a synergist in this action” ([11.1 Interactions of Skeletal Muscles, Their Fascicle Arrangement, and Their Lever Systems](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-1-interactions-of-skeletal-muscles-their-fascicle-arrangement-and-their-lever-systems), CC BY 4.0)
   > “synergist: muscle whose contraction helps a prime mover in an action” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Brachioradialis: Plain (drafted; grade 7.6; `content/FMA_38485/tier1.md`; also Right brachioradialis, Left brachioradialis)

1. The brachioradialis is a muscle that helps bend the elbow, a movement called forearm flexion. *(defines “forearm flexion”)*
   > “During forearm flexion—bending the elbow—the brachioradialis assists the brachialis.” ([11.1 Interactions of Skeletal Muscles, Their Fascicle Arrangement, and Their Lever Systems](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-1-interactions-of-skeletal-muscles-their-fascicle-arrangement-and-their-lever-systems), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle can bend the elbow quickly, or help lift a load slowly.
   > “brachioradialis: muscle that can flex the forearm quickly or help lift a load slowly” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. The brachioradialis runs from the arm to the radius, the forearm bone on the thumb side. *(defines “radius”)*
   > “brachioradialis (origin on the brachium or arm, insertion on the radius)” ([SEER Training Modules: Muscle Groups](https://training.seer.cancer.gov/anatomy/muscular/groups/), Free of copyright (NCI reuse policy))
   > “The radius runs parallel to the ulna, on the lateral (thumb) side of the forearm” ([8.2 Bones of the Upper Limb](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-2-bones-of-the-upper-limb), CC BY 4.0)

#### Coracobrachialis: Plain (drafted; grade 7.6; `content/FMA_37664/tier1.md`; also Right coracobrachialis, Left coracobrachialis)

1. The coracobrachialis flexes the arm, moving the arm forward at the shoulder. *(defines “flex”)*
   > “coracobrachialis: muscle that flexes and adducts the arm” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “For the upper limb, all anterior-going motions are flexion and all posterior-going motions are extension. These include anterior-posterior movements of the arm at the shoulder” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle also adducts the arm, bringing the arm down to the body's side. *(defines “adduct”)*
   > “Finally, the coracobrachialis flexes and adducts the arm.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “For example, abduction is raising the arm at the shoulder joint, moving it laterally away from the body, while adduction brings the arm down to the side of the body.” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
3. The coracobrachialis starts on the scapula, or shoulder blade. *(defines “scapula”)*
   > “The deltoid, subscapularis, supraspinatus, infraspinatus, teres major, teres minor, and coracobrachialis originate on the scapula.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “scapula: shoulder blade bone located on the posterior side of the shoulder” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. The muscle's other end attaches to the humerus, the upper arm bone. *(defines “humerus”)*
   > “A muscle arising from the coracoid process of the scapula, inserted in the medial border of the humerus, and acts to adduct and flex the arm.” ([NCIT:C53151 Coracobrachialis](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C53151), CC BY 4.0)
   > “humerus: single bone of the upper arm” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Digastric: Plain (drafted; grade 7.3; `content/FMA_46291/tier1.md`; also Right digastric, Left digastric)

1. Each digastric muscle lifts the hyoid bone, a U-shaped bone in the upper neck, when you swallow. *(defines “hyoid bone”)*
   > “digastric: muscle that has anterior and posterior bellies and elevates the hyoid bone and larynx when one swallows; it also depresses the mandible” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “hyoid bone: small, U-shaped bone located in upper neck that does not contact any other bone” ([Chapter 7 Key Terms (Axial Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/7-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. The digastric also lifts the larynx, or voice box, during a swallow. *(defines “larynx”)*
   > “digastric: muscle that has anterior and posterior bellies and elevates the hyoid bone and larynx when one swallows; it also depresses the mandible” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “The muscles of the anterior neck assist in deglutition (swallowing) and speech by controlling the positions of the larynx (voice box)” ([11.3 Axial Muscles of the Head, Neck, and Back](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-3-axial-muscles-of-the-head-neck-and-back), CC BY 4.0)
3. Each digastric lies under the mandible, the bone that forms the lower jaw, and pulls the jaw down. *(defines “mandible”)*
   > “A small, striated muscle located under the mandible. It arises from the mastoid process and extends to the symphysis menti, and functions to lower the mandible and elevation of the hyoid bone.” ([NCIT:C32462 Digastric Muscle](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C32462), CC BY 4.0)
   > “mandible: unpaired bone that forms the lower jaw bone; the only moveable bone of the skull” ([Chapter 7 Key Terms (Axial Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/7-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. Each digastric has two bellies, front and back; a belly is the bulky middle part of a muscle. *(defines “belly”)*
   > “digastric: muscle that has anterior and posterior bellies and elevates the hyoid bone and larynx when one swallows; it also depresses the mandible” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “belly: bulky central body of a muscle” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Extensor carpi radialis brevis: Plain (drafted; grade 7.6; `content/FMA_38497/tier1.md`; also Right extensor carpi radialis brevis, Left extensor carpi radialis brevis)

1. The extensor carpi radialis brevis extends the hand at the wrist, moving the hand backward. *(defines “extend”)*
   > “extensor carpi radialis brevis: muscle that extends and abducts the hand at the wrist” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “For the upper limb, all anterior-going motions are flexion and all posterior-going motions are extension. These include anterior-posterior movements of the arm at the shoulder, the forearm at the elbow, the hand at the wrist” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle also abducts the hand, moving the hand away from the body's midline. *(defines “abduct”)*
   > “extensor carpi radialis brevis: muscle that extends and abducts the hand at the wrist” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Similarly, abduction and adduction at the wrist moves the hand away from or toward the midline of the body.” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
3. The muscle runs from the humerus, the upper arm bone, along the back of the forearm. *(defines “humerus”)*
   > “The muscles in the superficial posterior compartment of the forearm (superficial posterior extensor compartment of the forearm) originate on the humerus. These are the extensor radialis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “humerus: single bone of the upper arm” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. The muscle's other end attaches to the third metacarpal, a long bone of the palm. *(defines “metacarpal”)*
   > “A muscle originating at the lateral epicondyle of the humerus and inserted at the third metacarpal bone” ([NCIT:C52911 Extensor Carpi Radialis Brevis](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C52911), CC BY 4.0)
   > “metacarpal bone: one of the five long bones that form the palm of the hand” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Extensor carpi ulnaris: Plain (drafted; grade 7.4; `content/FMA_38506/tier1.md`; also Right extensor carpi ulnaris, Left extensor carpi ulnaris)

1. The extensor carpi ulnaris extends the hand at the wrist, moving the hand backward. *(defines “extend”)*
   > “extensor carpi ulnaris: muscle that extends and adducts the hand” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “For the upper limb, all anterior-going motions are flexion and all posterior-going motions are extension. These include anterior-posterior movements of the arm at the shoulder, the forearm at the elbow, the hand at the wrist” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle also adducts the hand, moving the hand toward the body's midline. *(defines “adduct”)*
   > “extensor carpi ulnaris: muscle that extends and adducts the hand” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Similarly, abduction and adduction at the wrist moves the hand away from or toward the midline of the body.” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
3. The muscle runs from the humerus, the upper arm bone, along the back of the forearm. *(defines “humerus”)*
   > “muscles in the superficial posterior compartment of the forearm (superficial posterior extensor compartment of the forearm) originate on the humerus. These are the extensor radialis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis, extensor digitorum, extensor digiti minimi, and the extensor carpi ulnaris.” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “humerus: single bone of the upper arm” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. The muscle's other end attaches to the fifth metacarpal, a long bone of the palm. *(defines “metacarpal”)*
   > “A muscle originating at the humeral and ulnar heads and inserted at the base of the fifth metacarpal bone” ([NCIT:C52914 Extensor Carpi Ulnaris](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C52914), CC BY 4.0)
   > “metacarpal bone: one of the five long bones that form the palm of the hand” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Extensor digiti minimi: Plain (drafted; grade 7; `content/FMA_38503/tier1.md`; also Right extensor digiti minimi, Left extensor digiti minimi)

1. The extensor digiti minimi extends, or straightens, the little finger. *(defines “extend”)*
   > “extensor digiti minimi: muscle that extends the little finger” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “extension: movement in the sagittal plane that increases the angle of a joint (straightens the joint)” ([Chapter 9 Key Terms (Joints)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle lies on the back, or posterior side, of the forearm. *(defines “posterior”)*
   > “superficial posterior compartment of the forearm: extensor radialis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis, extensor digitorum, extensor digiti minimi, extensor carpi ulnaris, and their associated blood vessels and nerves” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “posterior: describes the back or direction toward the back of the body; also referred to as dorsal” ([Chapter 1 Key Terms (An Introduction to the Human Body)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/1-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. The muscle starts on the humerus, the upper arm bone. *(defines “humerus”)*
   > “The muscles in the superficial posterior compartment of the forearm (superficial posterior extensor compartment of the forearm) originate on the humerus. These are the extensor radialis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis, extensor digitorum, extensor digiti minimi” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “humerus: single bone of the upper arm” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Extensor digitorum: Plain (drafted; grade 7.2; `content/FMA_38500/tier1.md`; also Right extensor digitorum, Left extensor digitorum)

1. Each extensor digitorum extends, or straightens, the fingers and the hand at the wrist. *(defines “extend”)*
   > “whereas the extensor digitorum extends the fingers and the hand at the wrist” ([11.1 Interactions of Skeletal Muscles, Their Fascicle Arrangement, and Their Lever Systems](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-1-interactions-of-skeletal-muscles-their-fascicle-arrangement-and-their-lever-systems), CC BY 4.0)
   > “extension: movement in the sagittal plane that increases the angle of a joint (straightens the joint)” ([Chapter 9 Key Terms (Joints)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle lies near the surface at the back of the forearm, between the elbow and the wrist. *(defines “forearm”)*
   > “superficial posterior compartment of the forearm: extensor radialis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis, extensor digitorum, extensor digiti minimi, extensor carpi ulnaris, and their associated blood vessels and nerves” ([Chapter 11 Key Terms (The Muscular System)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
   > “forearm: region of the upper limb located between the elbow and wrist joints” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
3. The extensor digitorum starts on the humerus, the bone of the upper arm. *(defines “humerus”)*
   > “The muscles in the superficial posterior compartment of the forearm (superficial posterior extensor compartment of the forearm) originate on the humerus. These are the extensor radialis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis, extensor digitorum” ([11.5 Muscles of the Pectoral Girdle and Upper Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-5-muscles-of-the-pectoral-girdle-and-upper-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “humerus: single bone of the upper arm” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
4. The muscle's other end attaches to the phalanges, the bones of the fingers. *(defines “phalanges”)*
   > “inserted at the base of the proximal and middle phalanges as well as the base of the distal phalanges” ([NCIT:C52916 Extensor Digitorum Communis](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C52916), CC BY 4.0)
   > “phalanx bone of the hand: (plural = phalanges) one of the 14 bones that form the thumb and fingers” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)

#### Extensor digitorum longus: Plain (drafted; grade 8; `content/FMA_22534/tier1.md`; also Right extensor digitorum longus, Left extensor digitorum longus)

1. The extensor digitorum longus extends, or straightens, the second through fifth toes. *(defines “extend”)*
   > “A muscle originating at the lateral condyle of the tibia and the upper portion of the anterior margin of the fibula and inserted at the dorsal surface of the proximal, middle and distal phalanges of the second, third, fourth, and fifth toes which controls their extension.” ([NCIT:C52918 Extensor Digitorum Longus](http://purl.obolibrary.org/obo/NCIT_C52918), CC BY 4.0)
   > “extension: movement in the sagittal plane that increases the angle of a joint (straightens the joint)” ([Chapter 9 Key Terms (Joints)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
2. This muscle also helps raise the front of the foot, a movement called dorsiflexion. *(defines “dorsiflexion”)*
   > “The muscles in the anterior compartment of the leg: the tibialis anterior, a long and thick muscle on the lateral surface of the tibia, the extensor hallucis longus, deep under it, and the extensor digitorum longus, lateral to it, all contribute to raising the front of the foot when they contract.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “Lifting the front of the foot, so that the top of the foot moves toward the anterior leg is dorsiflexion” ([9.5 Types of Body Movements](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/9-5-types-of-body-movements), CC BY 4.0)
3. The extensor digitorum longus sits at the front of the leg, between the knee and the ankle. *(defines “leg”)*
   > “The muscles in the anterior compartment of the leg: the tibialis anterior, a long and thick muscle on the lateral surface of the tibia, the extensor hallucis longus, deep under it, and the extensor digitorum longus, lateral to it, all contribute to raising the front of the foot when they contract.” ([11.6 Appendicular Muscles of the Pelvic Girdle and Lower Limbs](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/11-6-appendicular-muscles-of-the-pelvic-girdle-and-lower-limbs), CC BY 4.0)
   > “leg: portion of the lower limb located between the knee and ankle joints” ([Chapter 8 Key Terms (The Appendicular Skeleton)](https://openstax.org/books/anatomy-and-physiology-2e/pages/8-key-terms), CC BY 4.0)
